I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no… I still bake sometimes, and I still check the telescope. So I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve abandoned everything.

Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing any activities that you used to enjoy, or do you find that you no longer take pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no… I still bake sometimes, and I still check the telescope. So I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve abandoned everything.\n\nBut I guess they don’t feel the same. Most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background, and even when I’m doing something I used to really enjoy, I don’t feel very light or excited about it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions a bit. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… I wouldn’t say I feel completely hopeless, but I do catch myself assuming things won’t really change much.

Like with the house, I kind of think it’s never going to feel fully settled. And I guess I also imagine I’m just going to keep functioning at this reduced level, if that makes sense. It makes it harder to plan things I used to look forward to, like trips or stargazing projects, because part of me thinks, “What’s the point, I probably won’t follow through anyway.”

I don’t know if that counts as hopeless exactly, but it’s there.

Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I don’t feel very refreshed. I can get through work and things around the house, mostly, but by the end there’s just not much left in me for anything else. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like hobbies, time with family, or other things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things—I go to the lab, I talk to people, I get through the day—so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. But most days there’s kind of this low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nThings I used to get a little lift from, like baking or checking the telescope, just don’t feel as… I don’t know, exciting or light as they used to. I still care about them, I think. It’s just harder to actually feel much from them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has this low mood been pretty much every day for at least two years, or is it more recent?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, it’s been a while. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’ve still been managing, but this kind of low baseline started not long after we moved into the older house. That was a little over two years ago now, so… I suppose it has been around that long. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do still get my work done and keep up with things at home, more or less. But I feel worn down almost all the time, even if I’ve slept enough or haven’t done anything especially demanding.\n\nBy the time I’ve handled the lab and then the house clutter and regular chores, I feel like there’s very little left in me. I’m not sure if that counts as “low energy” in the way you mean, but it does feel that way most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or depressed most days, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair. It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t get out of bed, so I sometimes feel a little dramatic calling it “depressed.” But most days there is this low, gray sort of mood in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab and talk to people and do what I need to do. I just don’t feel very light, I guess. Even things like baking or checking the telescope don’t really give me that spark the way they used to. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing any activities that you used to enjoy, or do you find that you no longer take pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no… I still bake sometimes, and I still check the telescope. So I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve abandoned everything.\n\nBut I guess they don’t feel the same. Most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background, and even when I’m doing something I used to really enjoy, I don’t feel very light or excited about it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions a bit. Is that what you mean by not taking pleasure?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest or pleasure in doing things, even things you used to really enjoy, not just a reduced feeling of excitement?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. It’s softer, I guess. I still care about those things, just not with the same pull or enjoyment. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you're not good enough, even when there's no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I can be hard on myself sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty for no reason, or like I’m a terrible person.\n\nMaybe I second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like a main thing. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns, like having trouble sleeping even when you’re tired, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. I mean, some nights are better than others, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m sleeping a lot more than usual or consistently lying awake even when I’m exhausted.\n\nAt least, nothing that stood out to me as a real pattern. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling hopeless about the future or like things won’t get better, even if you can’t point to a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t say I feel completely hopeless, but I do catch myself assuming things won’t really change much.\n\nLike with the house, I kind of think it’s never going to feel fully settled. And I guess I also imagine I’m just going to keep functioning at this reduced level, if that makes sense. It makes it harder to plan things I used to look forward to, like trips or stargazing projects, because part of me thinks, “What’s the point, I probably won’t follow through anyway.”\n\nI don’t know if that counts as hopeless exactly, but it’s there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think it’s been almost all activities, no. There are definitely things that feel harder to get into, or I don’t enjoy them as much as I think I should, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a time where nothing gave me any interest or pleasure.

I can still get some enjoyment from things, even if it’s not as easy or consistent. So… I think it’s more reduced than gone, if that’s a reasonable distinction.

Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing any activities that you used to enjoy, or do you find that you no longer take pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no… I still bake sometimes, and I still check the telescope. So I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve abandoned everything.\n\nBut I guess they don’t feel the same. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling hopeless about the future or like things won’t get better, even if you can’t point to a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t say I feel completely hopeless, but I do catch myself assuming things won’t really change much.\n\nLike with the house, I kind of think it’s never going to feel fully settled. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve just been feeling really worn down most of the time. Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I don’t feel very refreshed. I can get through work and things around the house, mostly, but by the end there’s just not much left in me for anything else. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like hobbies, time with family, or other things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do things—I go to the lab, I talk to people, I get through the day—so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. But most days there’s kind of this low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nThings I used to get a little lift from, like baking or checking the telescope, just don’t feel as… I don’t know, exciting or light as they used to. I still care about them, I think. It’s just harder to actually feel much from them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has this low mood been pretty much every day for at least two years, or is it more recent?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, it’s been a while. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’ve still been managing, but this kind of low baseline started not long after we moved into the older house. That was a little over two years ago now, so… I suppose it has been around that long. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do still get my work done and keep up with things at home, more or less. But I feel worn down almost all the time, even if I’ve slept enough or haven’t done anything especially demanding.\n\nBy the time I’ve handled the lab and then the house clutter and regular chores, I feel like there’s very little left in me. I’m not sure if that counts as “low energy” in the way you mean, but it does feel that way most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down or depressed most days, even if it's not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair. It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t get out of bed, so I sometimes feel a little dramatic calling it “depressed.” But most days there is this low, gray sort of mood in the background.\n\nI can still go to the lab and talk to people and do what I need to do. I just don’t feel very light, I guess. Even things like baking or checking the telescope don’t really give me that spark the way they used to. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing any activities that you used to enjoy, or do you find that you no longer take pleasure in them at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no… I still bake sometimes, and I still check the telescope. So I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve abandoned everything.\n\nBut I guess they don’t feel the same. Most days there’s this low, gray mood in the background, and even when I’m doing something I used to really enjoy, I don’t feel very light or excited about it. It’s more like I’m going through the motions a bit. Is that what you mean by not taking pleasure?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest or pleasure in doing things, even things you used to really enjoy, not just a reduced feeling of excitement?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I do still have interest in them in the sense that I choose to do them, and I don’t dislike them or anything.\n\nBut… if you mean whether they feel rewarding the way they used to, maybe not as much. It’s softer, I guess. I still care about those things, just not with the same pull or enjoyment. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you're not good enough, even when there's no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I can be hard on myself sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty for no reason, or like I’m a terrible person.\n\nMaybe I second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like a main thing. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns, like having trouble sleeping even when you’re tired, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. I mean, some nights are better than others, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m sleeping a lot more than usual or consistently lying awake even when I’m exhausted.\n\nAt least, nothing that stood out to me as a real pattern. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling hopeless about the future or like things won’t get better, even if you can’t point to a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I wouldn’t say I feel completely hopeless, but I do catch myself assuming things won’t really change much.\n\nLike with the house, I kind of think it’s never going to feel fully settled. And I guess I also imagine I’m just going to keep functioning at this reduced level, if that makes sense. It makes it harder to plan things I used to look forward to, like trips or stargazing projects, because part of me thinks, “What’s the point, I probably won’t follow through anyway.”\n\nI don’t know if that counts as hopeless exactly, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt little or no interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just a reduction in how much you enjoy them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been almost all activities, no. There are definitely things that feel harder to get into, or I don’t enjoy them as much as I think I should, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a time where nothing gave me any interest or pleasure.\n\nI can still get some enjoyment from things, even if it’s not as easy or consistent. So… I think it’s more reduced than gone, if that’s a reasonable distinction.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, my appetite fluctuates a little depending on the day, but I haven’t noticed a clear change over the past couple of years.\n\nAnd my weight’s been pretty much in the same general range as far as I know. Nothing that felt unintentional or concerning. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not almost every day. I mean, I can have slower days where I’m kind of dragging, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slowly in a way other people would see, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAnd I don’t think I’m especially restless either. I can sit still okay. So… no, I don’t think that one really fits.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, even things that normally bring you satisfaction?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had days where it was absolutely no interest or pleasure. I mean, some things can feel kind of muted or like I have to make more effort to enjoy them, but not completely gone.\n\nEven things I like can still feel somewhat satisfying once I get into them. So I’d say reduced sometimes, maybe, but not “absolutely none,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take any real pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even once you start doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “no longer” or “any real pleasure,” no. There are definitely times where I’m kind of lukewarm about things, or I don’t feel as excited as I think I should, but once I actually start doing something I used to like, I can usually still get something out of it.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s completely gone. Maybe just less strong sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even for a period of time, where even starting activities felt like too much effort and nothing brought any satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that total way. There have been times where starting something feels like a lot, and I might put it off, but once I do start, I can usually get at least a little satisfaction from it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I couldn’t enjoy anything at all. That doesn’t really sound accurate for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, your low mood is present for the majority of the day, even from the moment you wake up?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still get up and go to the lab and talk to people and do what I need to do.\n\nBut there’s kind of this low, gray feeling in the background a lot of the day. Even things I used to feel more light or excited about, like baking or checking the telescope, don’t have that same lift to them. Sometimes it’s there pretty much from when I wake up, but I still function, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there have been times when activities you used to enjoy, like baking or checking the telescope, felt completely unrewarding or gave you no pleasure at all, even while you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think completely unrewarding, no. I mean, there are definitely times where I’m kind of going through the motions, and it doesn’t feel as satisfying as I wish it did.\n\nBut even then, if I’m baking or looking at something through the telescope, there’s usually… something there. Maybe not a big feeling, but a little bit of interest or comfort. So I don’t think “no pleasure at all” would be the right way to put it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt no interest or pleasure at all in activities you used to enjoy, not just a lower level of enjoyment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been “no interest or pleasure at all,” no. It’s more like the volume is turned down, if that makes sense.\n\nI might not feel as excited to start, or it might feel a little flat compared to how it used to, but once I’m doing it there’s usually still some small enjoyment or comfort there. So… I don’t think I’d describe it as completely gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  "candidates": ["F33.2"],
  "note": "The patient's symptoms have been present for over two years, with a persistent low mood, reduced interest and pleasure in activities, and a sense of fatigue or low energy. There is no evidence of complete loss of pleasure in previously enjoyed activities, no significant changes in sleep, appetite, or psychomotor symptoms, and no feelings of guilt or hopelessness severe enough to suggest a more severe or acute form of depression. This supports a diagnosis of persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia), or F33.2.",
  "is_final": true
